PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Data management
Topic: query performance on public tabular data
Question: How does variation in read-to-update ratio change the balance of query latency, update overhead and storage for indexes on public environmental measurement tables?
Rationale: An index can accelerate reads while imposing storage and update costs; a recommendation based only on query latency can therefore be misleading.
Contribution: Identify workload conditions where an indexing choice changes from a useful speed improvement to an avoidable maintenance cost.
Method: Replay matched workloads across read-to-update ratio with fixed index configurations, consistent warm-up and repeated runs on the same machine.
Comparison: Compare each configuration with an unindexed database and a fixed index set under identical query workloads.
Evaluation: Report median query time, update cost and storage by setting, separating read-heavy and update-heavy workload results.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Standardized runs improve comparability, while the fixed machine and workload family limit transfer to other installations.

PRIOR BROADER-IMPACT ACTIVITIES
No prior broader-impact activity reported.

BROADER-IMPACTS PLAN
Beneficiaries: Community learners seeking to interpret public data visualizations
Goal: Help participants recognize uncertainty and misleading comparisons in public data displays. The learning task is adapting an example.
Activity: Prepare a guided session using public datasets and contrasting examples of data visualizations.
Delivery: Seek a host at an open online learning group, offer a guided pilot activity and collect participant questions for a revised version.
Access: Offer the materials without a participation fee and provide worked examples suitable for learners using ordinary laptops or printed instructions.
Assessment: Record activity completion and questions about adapting an example; use participant comments to revise the examples and instructions after the pilot.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Scientific computing
Topic: reproducible processing of public scientific tables
Question: How does variation in missing-value conventions affect fault detection by lightweight checks in public transport analysis workflows?
Rationale: A workflow can run successfully while producing a misleading table; examining silent processing faults can distinguish successful execution from reliable reuse.
Contribution: Characterize which silent processing failures inexpensive checks detect and which require contextual knowledge beyond schema validation.
Method: Compare unchecked, schema-only and combined checks across missing-value conventions, using a fixed list of processing faults and expected table outputs.
Comparison: Compare unchecked workflows, schema-only checks and the combined check set on identical fault cases.
Evaluation: Report detected faults, false alarms and overhead by failure category, comparing the same fault cases across check sets.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Using shared fault cases permits comparisons, while a developer-selected fault list can still favor the implemented checks.

2022-01 | 24 hours | organized a series of student reproducibility practice activities. Delivered a sequence of activities, reviewed participant task responses and revised the materials using that feedback..

BROADER-IMPACTS PLAN
Beneficiaries: Instructors and students learning reproducible computational analysis
Goal: Make a small reproducible analysis example easier to use in introductory teaching. The learning task is adapting an example.
Activity: Create an openly licensed tutorial with runnable examples, dependency instructions and an instructor guide.
Delivery: Seek a host at a campus learning centre, pilot the activity with volunteer learners, revise the difficult steps and offer a follow-up session.
Access: Include text alternatives for figures, small downloadable inputs and instructions for running examples on ordinary laptops.
Assessment: Use a short task on adapting an example before and after the activity, inspect common mistakes and use optional feedback to revise the materials.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Programming languages
Topic: static checks for small programming exercises
Question: How does variation in identifier reuse affect false warnings from syntax-pattern checks on public list-processing example programs?
Rationale: A warning rule that matches a useful surface pattern can still generate false positives when surrounding program context changes its meaning.
Contribution: Identify contexts where additional syntax information reduces false warnings and document cases where that extra complexity still misses the intended distinction.
Method: Vary identifier reuse in examples reserved by program family, define labeling rules before tuning, and have a collaborator independently recheck a subset of warning labels.
Comparison: Use context-free pattern matching on the same manually checked examples as the reference method.
Evaluation: Report precision and recall by context with uncertainty intervals, adjudicate labeling disagreements, and evaluate on program families excluded from rule tuning.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Manual labels and template overlap can distort apparent gains; publish the labeling rules and repeat analysis after excluding near-duplicate program families.

PRIOR BROADER-IMPACT ACTIVITIES
No prior broader-impact activity reported.

BROADER-IMPACTS PLAN
Beneficiaries: Students beginning programming
Goal: Help beginning learners interpret and modify a small data-analysis example. The learning task is reproducing an example.
Activity: Develop a short hands-on programming workshop with worked examples and reusable exercises.
Delivery: Seek a host at a student-led computing club, pilot the activity with volunteer learners, revise the difficult steps and offer a follow-up session.
Access: Provide browser-based exercises and downloadable text instructions so a high-performance computer is unnecessary.
Assessment: Use a short task on reproducing an example before and after the activity, inspect common mistakes and use optional feedback to revise the materials.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Software engineering
Topic: test coverage for small open-source libraries
Question: How does variation in test execution budget affect fault detection by coverage-guided test selection on public numerical utility libraries?
Rationale: Coverage can increase without detecting additional faults, so a useful selection strategy must preserve defect detection while reducing execution cost.
Contribution: Characterize when coverage is a useful proxy for fault detection and identify failure categories where inexpensive test selection loses important evidence.
Method: Compare budget-matched coverage-guided and random subsets across test execution budget, define defect categories before selection, and reserve library versions for a transfer check.
Comparison: Use the full test suite as an unrestricted reference, and compare random selection with coverage-guided selection under matched execution budgets.
Evaluation: Report detection loss and runtime with repeated-selection intervals on held-out versions; separate real-defect results from any mutation-based supplementary check.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Defect availability can favor well-documented libraries; report inclusion rules and examine whether conclusions change when libraries are held out.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Scientific computing
Topic: reproducible processing of public scientific tables
Question: How does variation in schema changes affect fault detection by lightweight checks in public municipal-data analysis workflows?
Rationale: A workflow can run successfully while producing a misleading table; examining silent processing faults can distinguish successful execution from reliable reuse.
Contribution: Characterize which silent processing failures inexpensive checks detect and which require contextual knowledge beyond schema validation.
Method: Define fault categories before writing checks, vary schema changes, and reserve workflows and fault instances for evaluating schema-only and combined checks.
Comparison: Compare unchecked workflows, schema-only checks and the combined check set on identical fault cases.
Evaluation: Report detection and false-alarm rates on held-out workflows with category-specific intervals; test whether conclusions change when fault prevalence differs.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Injected faults need not match real processing failures; disclose the fault taxonomy and distinguish detection coverage from claims about complete analytical correctness.

Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Programming languages
Topic: static checks for small programming exercises
Question: How does variation in identifier reuse affect false warnings from syntax-pattern checks on public numeric-computation example programs?
Rationale: A warning rule that matches a useful surface pattern can still generate false positives when surrounding program context changes its meaning.
Contribution: Identify contexts where additional syntax information reduces false warnings and document cases where that extra complexity still misses the intended distinction.
Method: Vary identifier reuse in examples reserved by program family, define labeling rules before tuning, and have a collaborator independently recheck a subset of warning labels.
Comparison: Use context-free pattern matching on the same manually checked examples as the reference method.
Evaluation: Report precision and recall by context with uncertainty intervals, adjudicate labeling disagreements, and evaluate on program families excluded from rule tuning.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Manual labels and template overlap can distort apparent gains; publish the labeling rules and repeat analysis after excluding near-duplicate program families.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Applied machine learning
Topic: forecasting from public environmental time series
Question: How does variation in seasonal variation affect imputation and short-horizon forecasting on public river-level measurement series?
Rationale: A low average forecast error may hide failure during extended outages; comparing missingness patterns can clarify when imputation ceases to be useful.
Contribution: Characterize conditions where simple imputation preserves forecast usefulness and where uncertainty or long outages undermine that conclusion.
Method: Compare several imputation methods across seasonal variation, retain chronological splits, and evaluate separately by sensor and missingness pattern. Fill missing historical inputs using past observations only, then forecast one step ahead with lagged-input ridge regression fitted only on earlier observations.
Comparison: Compare the resulting ridge forecasts with seasonal-naive forecasts on the same test dates. Also apply the same ridge model to unaltered historical inputs as a reference.
Evaluation: Report forecast errors by sensor and outage pattern over several windows, including the error of the seasonal-naive reference.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Chronological evaluation avoids future-data leakage, while simulated missingness may differ from the process causing actual sensor failures.

Area: Natural language processing
Topic: classification of short public text documents
Question: How does variation in training-set size change the relative performance of linear and compact encoder classifiers on public product-review text?
Rationale: Overall accuracy can conceal failures on short documents and rare labels, making it difficult to decide when additional training data help those groups.

Method: Compare the classifiers under matched training budgets across training-set size, separate near-duplicates before splitting, and repeat training on multiple held-out partitions.
Comparison: Compare against a majority-class baseline and a full-training-set linear classifier using the same held-out documents.
Evaluation: Report macro F-score, label-specific errors and training cost with repeated-split uncertainty estimates; test whether the pattern transfers to a separately held-out collection.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Label definitions and duplicate detection can change the apparent effect; retain per-collection results and test the result under alternative deduplication rules.

Question: How does variation in query selectivity change the balance of query latency, update overhead and storage for indexes on public transit timetable tables?
Rationale: An index can accelerate reads while imposing storage and update costs; a recommendation based only on query latency can therefore be misleading.
Contribution: Identify workload conditions where an indexing choice changes from a useful speed improvement to an avoidable maintenance cost.
Method: Vary query selectivity under matched database states, randomize workload order, standardize warm-up, and reserve workload mixes for checking the indexing tradeoff.

Evaluation: Report median and tail latency, update overhead and storage with run-to-run intervals; test whether the inferred tradeoff persists on held-out workload mixes.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Cache effects and skewed inputs may change the preferred index; repeat key comparisons with cold starts and altered data skew.

Area: Algorithms
Topic: path search on public transportation graphs
Question: How does variation in query repetition affect the total cost of preprocessed shortest-path queries on public regional rail networks?
Rationale: Graph preprocessing can reduce query time but becomes expensive when routes change, so query speed alone does not describe the useful operating range.
Contribution: Map the conditions under which preprocessing repays its construction cost while retaining correct route costs after the tested changes.
Method: Vary query repetition across held-out network regions, compare total preprocessing and query cost, and test path-cost agreement after each controlled route change.
Comparison: Use unmodified Dijkstra search on the same graphs and query pairs as the correctness and timing baseline.
Evaluation: Report correctness failures and total cost with repeated-query intervals; locate break-even query counts and verify them on held-out network regions.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Controlled changes do not reproduce live transport disruptions; report sensitivity to changed edges and separate correctness from timing conclusions.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Data management
Topic: query performance on public tabular data
Question: How does variation in table-size variation change the balance of query latency, update overhead and storage for indexes on public bibliographic tables?
Rationale: An index can accelerate reads while imposing storage and update costs; a recommendation based only on query latency can therefore be misleading.

Method: Replay matched workloads across table-size variation with fixed index configurations, consistent warm-up and repeated runs on the same machine.

Evaluation: Report median query time, update cost and storage by setting, separating read-heavy and update-heavy workload results.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Standardized runs improve comparability, while the fixed machine and workload family limit transfer to other installations.

Topic: forecasting from public environmental time series
Question: How does variation in outage duration affect imputation and short-horizon forecasting on public air-quality sensor series?

Comparison: Compare the resulting ridge forecasts with seasonal-naive forecasts on the same test dates.
Evaluation: Summarize mean absolute error over the selected test period and inspect example forecasts near missing observations.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Algorithms
Topic: path search on public transportation graphs
Question: How does variation in network sparsity affect the total cost of preprocessed shortest-path queries on public urban bus networks?
Rationale: Graph preprocessing can reduce query time but becomes expensive when routes change, so query speed alone does not describe the useful operating range.
Contribution: Map the conditions under which preprocessing repays its construction cost while retaining correct route costs after the tested changes.
Method: Vary network sparsity across held-out network regions, compare total preprocessing and query cost, and test path-cost agreement after each controlled route change.
Comparison: Use unmodified Dijkstra search on the same graphs and query pairs as the correctness and timing baseline.
Evaluation: Report correctness failures and total cost with repeated-query intervals; locate break-even query counts and verify them on held-out network regions.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Controlled changes do not reproduce live transport disruptions; report sensitivity to changed edges and separate correctness from timing conclusions.

Area: Data management
Topic: query performance on public tabular data
Question: How does variation in table-size variation change the balance of query latency, update overhead and storage for indexes on public transit timetable tables?
Rationale: An index can accelerate reads while imposing storage and update costs; a recommendation based only on query latency can therefore be misleading.
Contribution: Identify workload conditions where an indexing choice changes from a useful speed improvement to an avoidable maintenance cost.
Method: Vary table-size variation under matched database states, randomize workload order, standardize warm-up, and reserve workload mixes for checking the indexing tradeoff.
Comparison: Compare each configuration with an unindexed database and a fixed index set under identical query workloads.
Evaluation: Report median and tail latency, update overhead and storage with run-to-run intervals; test whether the inferred tradeoff persists on held-out workload mixes.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Cache effects and skewed inputs may change the preferred index; repeat key comparisons with cold starts and altered data skew.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Scientific computing
Topic: reproducible processing of public scientific tables
Question: How does variation in unit inconsistencies affect fault detection by lightweight checks in public municipal-data analysis workflows?
Rationale: A workflow can run successfully while producing a misleading table; examining silent processing faults can distinguish successful execution from reliable reuse.
Contribution: Characterize which silent processing failures inexpensive checks detect and which require contextual knowledge beyond schema validation.
Method: Define fault categories before writing checks, vary unit inconsistencies, and reserve workflows and fault instances for evaluating schema-only and combined checks.
Comparison: Compare unchecked workflows, schema-only checks and the combined check set on identical fault cases.

Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Injected faults need not match real processing failures; disclose the fault taxonomy and distinguish detection coverage from claims about complete analytical correctness.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Software engineering
Topic: test coverage for small open-source libraries
Question: How does variation in library version change affect fault detection by coverage-guided test selection on public parsing utilities?
Rationale: Coverage can increase without detecting additional faults, so a useful selection strategy must preserve defect detection while reducing execution cost.
Contribution: Characterize when coverage is a useful proxy for fault detection and identify failure categories where inexpensive test selection loses important evidence.
Method: Compare budget-matched coverage-guided and random subsets across library version change, define defect categories before selection, and reserve library versions for a transfer check.
Comparison: Use the full test suite as an unrestricted reference, and compare random selection with coverage-guided selection under matched execution budgets.
Evaluation: Report detection loss and runtime with repeated-selection intervals on held-out versions; separate real-defect results from any mutation-based supplementary check.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Defect availability can favor well-documented libraries; report inclusion rules and examine whether conclusions change when libraries are held out.

Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: A single split and transformed-only evaluation can obscure losses on ordinary images and variation between training runs.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Applied machine learning
Topic: forecasting from public environmental time series
Question: How does variation in missing-observation frequency affect imputation and short-horizon forecasting on public rainfall measurement series?
Rationale: A low average forecast error may hide failure during extended outages; comparing missingness patterns can clarify when imputation ceases to be useful.
Contribution: Characterize conditions where simple imputation preserves forecast usefulness and where uncertainty or long outages undermine that conclusion.
Method: Compare several imputation methods across missing-observation frequency, retain chronological splits, and evaluate separately by sensor and missingness pattern. Fill missing historical inputs using past observations only, then forecast one step ahead with lagged-input ridge regression fitted only on earlier observations.
Comparison: Compare the resulting ridge forecasts with seasonal-naive forecasts on the same test dates. Also apply the same ridge model to unaltered historical inputs as a reference.
Evaluation: Report forecast errors by sensor and outage pattern over several windows, including the error of the seasonal-naive reference.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Chronological evaluation avoids future-data leakage, while simulated missingness may differ from the process causing actual sensor failures.

Area: Software engineering
Topic: test coverage for small open-source libraries
Question: How does variation in library version change affect fault detection by coverage-guided test selection on public file-format utilities?
Rationale: Coverage can increase without detecting additional faults, so a useful selection strategy must preserve defect detection while reducing execution cost.
Contribution: Characterize when coverage is a useful proxy for fault detection and identify failure categories where inexpensive test selection loses important evidence.
Method: Compare coverage-guided and random test subsets under matched budgets, varying library version change across several library versions with reproducible defects.
Comparison: Use the full test suite as an unrestricted reference, and compare random selection with coverage-guided selection under matched execution budgets.
Evaluation: Report defect detection and runtime by version and defect type, repeating the random selection to estimate its variability.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Repeated random selection helps compare methods, while results can still depend on which library versions are included.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Programming languages
Topic: static checks for small programming exercises
Question: How does variation in helper-function boundaries affect false warnings from syntax-pattern checks on public file-processing example programs?
Rationale: A warning rule that matches a useful surface pattern can still generate false positives when surrounding program context changes its meaning.
Contribution: Identify contexts where additional syntax information reduces false warnings and document cases where that extra complexity still misses the intended distinction.
Method: Vary helper-function boundaries in examples reserved by program family, define labeling rules before tuning, and have a collaborator independently recheck a subset of warning labels.

Evaluation: Report precision and recall by context with uncertainty intervals, adjudicate labeling disagreements, and evaluate on program families excluded from rule tuning.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Manual labels and template overlap can distort apparent gains; publish the labeling rules and repeat analysis after excluding near-duplicate program families.

Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.

Area: Data management
Topic: query performance on public tabular data
Question: How does variation in query selectivity change the balance of query latency, update overhead and storage for indexes on public municipal service tables?
Rationale: An index can accelerate reads while imposing storage and update costs; a recommendation based only on query latency can therefore be misleading.
Contribution: Identify workload conditions where an indexing choice changes from a useful speed improvement to an avoidable maintenance cost.
Method: Replay matched workloads across query selectivity with fixed index configurations, consistent warm-up and repeated runs on the same machine.

Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Standardized runs improve comparability, while the fixed machine and workload family limit transfer to other installations.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Algorithms
Topic: path search on public transportation graphs
Question: How does variation in localized route changes affect the total cost of preprocessed shortest-path queries on public urban bus networks?

Area: Software engineering
Topic: test coverage for small open-source libraries
Question: How does variation in defect type affect fault detection by coverage-guided test selection on public numerical utility libraries?
Rationale: Coverage can increase without detecting additional faults, so a useful selection strategy must preserve defect detection while reducing execution cost.
Contribution: Characterize when coverage is a useful proxy for fault detection and identify failure categories where inexpensive test selection loses important evidence.
Method: Compare coverage-guided and random test subsets under matched budgets, varying defect type across several library versions with reproducible defects.
Comparison: Use the full test suite as an unrestricted reference, and compare random selection with coverage-guided selection under matched execution budgets.
Evaluation: Report defect detection and runtime by version and defect type, repeating the random selection to estimate its variability.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Repeated random selection helps compare methods, while results can still depend on which library versions are included.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Applied machine learning
Topic: forecasting from public environmental time series
Question: How does variation in outage duration affect imputation and short-horizon forecasting on public temperature sensor series?
Rationale: A low average forecast error may hide failure during extended outages; comparing missingness patterns can clarify when imputation ceases to be useful.
Contribution: Characterize conditions where simple imputation preserves forecast usefulness and where uncertainty or long outages undermine that conclusion.
Method: Vary outage duration across chronological evaluation windows, preserve a final holdout period, and compare imputation with seasonal-naive and unaltered-input reference forecasts. Fill missing historical inputs using past observations only, then forecast one step ahead with lagged-input ridge regression fitted only on earlier observations.
Comparison: Compare the resulting ridge forecasts with seasonal-naive forecasts on the same test dates. Also apply the same ridge model to unaltered historical inputs as a reference.
Evaluation: Report sensor-level error distributions and uncertainty across windows; test whether conclusions hold for long contiguous outages in the final holdout period.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Observed sensor failures can have different causes from injected missingness; compare several patterns and avoid interpreting associations as causal outage effects.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Data management
Topic: query performance on public tabular data
Question: How does variation in query selectivity change the balance of query latency, update overhead and storage for indexes on public bibliographic tables?
Rationale: An index can accelerate reads while imposing storage and update costs; a recommendation based only on query latency can therefore be misleading.
Contribution: Identify workload conditions where an indexing choice changes from a useful speed improvement to an avoidable maintenance cost.
Method: Vary query selectivity under matched database states, randomize workload order, standardize warm-up, and reserve workload mixes for checking the indexing tradeoff.
Comparison: Compare each configuration with an unindexed database and a fixed index set under identical query workloads.
Evaluation: Report median and tail latency, update overhead and storage with run-to-run intervals; test whether the inferred tradeoff persists on held-out workload mixes.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Cache effects and skewed inputs may change the preferred index; repeat key comparisons with cold starts and altered data skew.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Algorithms
Topic: path search on public transportation graphs
Question: How does variation in localized route changes affect the total cost of preprocessed shortest-path queries on public regional rail networks?
Rationale: Graph preprocessing can reduce query time but becomes expensive when routes change, so query speed alone does not describe the useful operating range.
Contribution: Map the conditions under which preprocessing repays its construction cost while retaining correct route costs after the tested changes.
Method: Vary localized route changes across held-out network regions, compare total preprocessing and query cost, and test path-cost agreement after each controlled route change.
Comparison: Use unmodified Dijkstra search on the same graphs and query pairs as the correctness and timing baseline.
Evaluation: Report correctness failures and total cost with repeated-query intervals; locate break-even query counts and verify them on held-out network regions.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Controlled changes do not reproduce live transport disruptions; report sensitivity to changed edges and separate correctness from timing conclusions.

Topic: static checks for small programming exercises
Question: How does variation in nesting depth affect false warnings from syntax-pattern checks on public numeric-computation example programs?

Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Manual labels and template overlap can distort apparent gains; publish the labeling rules and repeat analysis after excluding near-duplicate program families.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Programming languages
Topic: static checks for small programming exercises
Question: How does variation in helper-function boundaries affect false warnings from syntax-pattern checks on public list-processing example programs?
Rationale: A warning rule that matches a useful surface pattern can still generate false positives when surrounding program context changes its meaning.
Contribution: Identify contexts where additional syntax information reduces false warnings and document cases where that extra complexity still misses the intended distinction.
Method: Vary helper-function boundaries in examples reserved by program family, define labeling rules before tuning, and have a collaborator independently recheck a subset of warning labels.
Comparison: Use context-free pattern matching on the same manually checked examples as the reference method.
Evaluation: Report precision and recall by context with uncertainty intervals, adjudicate labeling disagreements, and evaluate on program families excluded from rule tuning.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Manual labels and template overlap can distort apparent gains; publish the labeling rules and repeat analysis after excluding near-duplicate program families.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Scientific computing
Topic: reproducible processing of public scientific tables
Question: How does variation in missing-value conventions affect fault detection by lightweight checks in public environmental analysis workflows?
Rationale: A workflow can run successfully while producing a misleading table; examining silent processing faults can distinguish successful execution from reliable reuse.
Contribution: Characterize which silent processing failures inexpensive checks detect and which require contextual knowledge beyond schema validation.
Method: Define fault categories before writing checks, vary missing-value conventions, and reserve workflows and fault instances for evaluating schema-only and combined checks.
Comparison: Compare unchecked workflows, schema-only checks and the combined check set on identical fault cases.
Evaluation: Report detection and false-alarm rates on held-out workflows with category-specific intervals; test whether conclusions change when fault prevalence differs.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Injected faults need not match real processing failures; disclose the fault taxonomy and distinguish detection coverage from claims about complete analytical correctness.

PROPOSED RESEARCH
Area: Natural language processing
Topic: classification of short public text documents
Question: How does variation in rare-label frequency change the relative performance of linear and compact encoder classifiers on public news-topic text?
Rationale: Overall accuracy can conceal failures on short documents and rare labels, making it difficult to decide when additional training data help those groups.
Contribution: Identify conditions where the encoder's additional training cost improves minority-label performance, and where the simpler classifier retains comparable performance.
Method: Compare the classifiers under matched training budgets across rare-label frequency, separate near-duplicates before splitting, and repeat training on multiple held-out partitions.
Comparison: Compare against a majority-class baseline and a full-training-set linear classifier using the same held-out documents.
Evaluation: Report macro F-score, label-specific errors and training cost with repeated-split uncertainty estimates; test whether the pattern transfers to a separately held-out collection.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Label definitions and duplicate detection can change the apparent effect; retain per-collection results and test the result under alternative deduplication rules.

Area: Computer vision
Topic: small-image classification under input changes
Question: How does variation in brightness variation affect the benefit and cost of training augmentation on public texture image collections?
Rationale: An augmentation can improve transformed-image accuracy while harming unchanged images, making aggregate accuracy an incomplete guide to robustness.
Contribution: Map where an augmentation improves transformed-image performance without unacceptable loss on unchanged inputs, including settings where the tradeoff reverses.
Method: Vary brightness variation under matched budgets and repeated training seeds, reserve transformation intensities for evaluation, and separate tuning images from the final test set.
Comparison: Compare with an unaugmented classifier and a fixed combined-augmentation baseline using the same train and test split.
Evaluation: Report paired accuracy differences and repeated-seed intervals for unchanged and held-out transformations; examine whether improvements persist beyond the tuning intensities.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Artificial transformations approximate only some imaging changes; distinguish tested perturbation tolerance from claims about deployment conditions or general visual robustness.

Question: How does variation in brightness variation affect the benefit and cost of training augmentation on public small-object image collections?
Rationale: An augmentation can improve transformed-image accuracy while harming unchanged images, making aggregate accuracy an incomplete guide to robustness.
Contribution: Map where an augmentation improves transformed-image performance without unacceptable loss on unchanged inputs, including settings where the tradeoff reverses.
Method: Vary brightness variation under matched budgets and repeated training seeds, reserve transformation intensities for evaluation, and separate tuning images from the final test set.
Comparison: Compare with an unaugmented classifier and a fixed combined-augmentation baseline using the same train and test split.
Evaluation: Report paired accuracy differences and repeated-seed intervals for unchanged and held-out transformations; examine whether improvements persist beyond the tuning intensities.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: Artificial transformations approximate only some imaging changes; distinguish tested perturbation tolerance from claims about deployment conditions or general visual robustness.

Area: Programming languages
Topic: static checks for small programming exercises
Question: How does variation in nesting depth affect false warnings from syntax-pattern checks on public numeric-computation example programs?
Rationale: A warning rule that matches a useful surface pattern can still generate false positives when surrounding program context changes its meaning.
Contribution: Identify contexts where additional syntax information reduces false warnings and document cases where that extra complexity still misses the intended distinction.
Method: Compare context-aware and pattern-only checks across nesting depth, manually check example labels, and retain a separate evaluation subset.
Comparison: Use context-free pattern matching on the same manually checked examples as the reference method.
Evaluation: Report precision and recall on the evaluation subset, separating warning categories and documenting ambiguous labels.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: A separate evaluation subset limits direct tuning effects, while related program templates may still occur on both sides of the split.

Comparison: Use the stated reference approach on the same selected inputs, with the available implementation settings held fixed during the comparison.
Evaluation: Report transformed-image accuracy from the chosen split and show representative misclassified images for each setting.
Feasibility: Use small public inputs and university computing available through the department; schedule a pilot, comparisons and a written report within six months.
Limitations: A single split and transformed-only evaluation can obscure losses on ordinary images and variation between training runs.
